## Document 007: 115年9月海關進出口貿易初步統計 (2026-10-08)

115 年 9 月海關進出口貿易初步統計 
一、進出口貿易變動 
（一）出口 
9 月出口 872.2 億美元，創歷年單月新高，較上月增 5.9%，較上年
同月增 60.9%(按新臺幣計算增 68.2%)；第3 季出口 2,449.2 億美元，
較上年同季增 44.6%；累計 1 至 9 月 6,615.5 億美元，較上年同期增
46.2%(按新臺幣計算增 49.1%)。 
（二）進口 
9 月進口 635.9 億美元，亦創歷年單月新高，較上月增 5.8%，較上
年同月增 51.7%(按新臺幣計算增 58.6%)；第 3 季進口 1,818.0 億美
元，較上年同季增 44.3%；累計 1 至 9 月 5,010.0 億美元，較上年同
期增 41.3%(按新臺幣計算增 43.8%)。 
（三）出入超 
9 月出超 236.3 億美元，為單月新高，較上年同月增 113.4 億美元；
累計 1 至 9 月出超 1,605.5 億美元，較上年同期增 625.4 億美元。

二、出口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
由於創新科技應用商機持續擴展，推升相關供應鏈出貨動能，加上
消費性電子新品備貨需求與產品價格上漲效應之挹注， 9 月出口較
上年同月增 330.0 億美元(+60.9%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 資通與視聽產品出口 391.3 億美元(歷年單月次高)， 較上年同月增
198.6 億美元(+1.0 倍)， 其中電腦及其附屬單元增157.4 億美元(+1.1
倍)、儲存媒體增 17.6 億美元(+2.2 倍)、交換器及路由器增 10.5
億美元(+1.1 倍)。 
2. 電子零組件出口 311.3 億美元(歷年單月次高)， 較上年同月增97.1
億美元(+45.3%)，其中積體電路增 93.9 億美元(+46.1%)、印刷電
路增 1.4 億美元(+33.7%)、 電容器及電阻器增0.8 億美元(+32.0%)。 
3. 礦產品出口 22.6 億美元，較上年同月增 10.5 億美元(+86.4%)，其
中石油煉製品增 10.1 億美元(+99.0%)。 
4. 機械出口 27.4 億美元(歷年單月次高)，較上年同月增 6.4 億美元
(+30.7%)，其中生產半導體等機械增 2.7 億美元(+61.0%)、未列名
具特殊功能機械增 0.6 億美元(+73.6%)。 
5. 基本金屬及其製品出口 26.9 億美元，較上年同月增 5.0 億美元
(+22.9%)，其中銅及其製品增 2.5 億美元(+65.1%)、鋼鐵及其製品
增 1.4 億美元(+11.7%)。

（二）與上年同期比較 
1. 第 3 季出口較上年同季增 755.9 億美元(+44.6%)，以資通與視聽產
品增 367.6 億美元(+55.0%)、電子零組件增 308.4 億美元(+51.2%)
增加較多。 
2. 累計 1 至 9 月出口較上年同期增 2,089.8 億美元(+46.2%)，其中資
通與視聽產品增 1,222.2 億美元(+72.4%)、電子零組件增 726.6 億
美元(+45.7%)。

等出現結構性轉變，爰另予併計。

三、進口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
隨 AI 產業鏈全球分工與出口引申需求擴增，電子零組件、資通產品
及半導體設備進口維持強勁，加上國際農工原料行情處於高檔，9
月進口較上年同月增 216.7 億美元(+51.7%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 農工原料進口440.5 億美元， 較上年同月增163.9億美元(+59.3%)，
其中電子零組件增 112.6 億美元(+82.5%)、電腦之零附件增 19.4
億美元(+1.5 倍)、礦產品增 17.1 億美元(+38.9%)。 
2. 資本設備進口 128.0 億美元， 較上年同月增 36.0 億美元(+39.1%)，
其中資通設備增 18.4 億美元(+75.0%)、半導體設備增 12.4 億美元
(+34.9%)，交通運輸設備減 1.4 億美元(-46.4%)。 
3. 消費品進口 62.1 億美元，較上年同月增 16.5 億美元(+36.0%)，其
中固態非揮發性儲存裝置增 16.4 億美元(+5.3 倍)、筆電增 0.5 億
美元(+43.8%)，藥粧用品減 1.2 億美元(-16.6%)。

四、主要出口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
9 月對東協出口創新高，對美國、陸港為單月次高 
1. 對美國出口 274.6 億美元，較上年同月增 141.5 億美元(+1.1 倍)，
其中資通與視聽產品增 124.1 億美元(+1.3 倍)、 電子零組件增10.1
億美元(+1.3 倍)、機械增 2.2 億美元(+42.3%)。 
2. 對中國大陸與香港出口 206.2 億美元，較上年同月增 47.8 億美元
(+30.2%)，其中電子零組件增 34.9 億美元(+30.5%)、資通與視聽
產品增 5.7 億美元(+39.0%)、機械增 1.9 億美元(+43.7%)。 
3. 對東協出口 188.8 億美元，較上年同月增 91.2 億美元(+93.4%)，
其中資通與視聽產品增 49.7 億美元(+2.2 倍)、電子零組件增 32.0
億美元(+63.3%)、礦產品增 4.8 億美元(+1.4 倍)。 
4. 對歐洲出口 45.3 億美元，較上年同月增 7.7 億美元(+20.6%)，其
中資通與視聽產品增 5.7 億美元(+34.1%)、電子零組件增 2.2 億美
元(+56.6%)，基本金屬及其製品減 0.8 億美元(-20.5%)。 
5. 對日本出口 30.7 億美元，較上年同月增 3.6 億美元(+13.4%)，其
中電子零組件增 1.5 億美元(+11.0%)、基本金屬及其製品增 0.6 億
美元(+32.5%)、機械增 0.5 億美元(+29.1%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 9 月對美國出口 2,077.8 億美元，較上年同期增 773.1 億美
元(+59.3%)，對中國大陸與香港出口 1,600.9 億美元，增 355.3 億美
元(+28.5%)， 對東協出口1,339.6 億美元 ， 增463.6 億美元(+52.9%)，
對歐洲出口 421.8 億美元 ， 增138.4 億美元(+48.8%)， 對日本出口277.7
億美元，增 58.3 億美元(+26.6%)。

五、主要進口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
9 月自中國大陸與香港進口 135.0 億美元 ， 較上年同月增50.1 億美元
(+59.1%)，自南韓進口 116.3 億美元，增52.6 億美元(+82.5%)，自東
協進口 106.0 億美元，增 52.6 億美元(+98.4%)，自日本進口 64.3 億
美元，增 17.7 億美元(+38.0%)，自歐洲進口 50.2 億美元，增 3.5 億
美元(+7.4%)，自美國進口 48.1 億美元，增 6.5 億美元(+15.5%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 9 月自主要國家/地區進口與上年同期比較 ， 自東協增77.6%、
自南韓增 65.8%、自中國大陸與香港增 42.8%、自日本增 25.7%、自
美國增 21.4%、自歐洲增 10.8%。

六、主要國家/地區貿易概況 
（一）主要國家/地區今年 1-8 月外貿景氣普遍走升，以南韓(1-9 月)出口年
增 56.8%最為強勁，香港、新加坡亦分別年增 49.0%、39.8%；中國
大陸、美國出口分別年增 19.3%及 14.6%，德國(1-7 月)、日本各增
9.7%、8.3%。 
（二）主要國家/地區進口同步上揚，累計 1-8 月以香港進口年增 50.5%較
突出，新加坡、中國大陸分別年增 37.3%、27.0%，南韓(1-9 月)、德
國(1-7 月)分別增 19.7%、10.0%，日本、美國各增 7.8%、4.2%。

### Page 7

7
圖 5 2026 年主要國家/地區出進口年增率
七、綜合分析
（一）受惠於人工智慧(AI)、高效能運算及雲端服務等應用商機持續擴展，
加以步入傳統外銷旺季 、產品價格上漲效應，9 月出口 872.2 億美元，
年增 60.9%，連續 35 個月正成長；進口635.9 億美元，年增51.7%，
主因 AI 產業鏈國際分工與出口引申需求擴張，及國際原物料行情居
高；出、進口值及出超 236.3 億美元齊創單月新高。累計 1 至 9 月出
口年增 46.2%、進口增 41.3%。
（二）9 月主要貨品出口普遍較上年同月成長，以資通與視聽產品增 1.0 倍
最佳，包括顯示卡、伺服器、儲存媒體、 交換器及路由器等出口均
呈倍數增加 ；其次為礦產品增 86.4%，主因全球油品供應偏緊支撐價
格維持高檔；電子零組件隨 AI 應用及消費性電子新品備貨需求升溫，
增 45.3%，機械、電機產品亦各增 3 成上下。累計 1 至 9 月資通與視
聽產品、電子零組件出口併計增 59.5%，占總出口比重 79.0%。

（三）主要市場方面，9 月對美國、東協出口值分創歷年單月次高、新高，
年增率各為 1.1 倍及 93.4%，合計對總出口增加貢獻達 7 成；對中國
大陸與香港、歐洲、日本出口各年增 30.2%、20.6%、13.4%。累計 1
至 9 月對五大市場出口值皆為歷年同期最高，其中對美國、東協均
提前刷新全年紀錄 ， 分別年增59.3%、52.9%，對歐洲出口年增48.8%，
對陸港、日本各增 28.5%、26.6%。
（四）展望未來，全球貿易政策及地緣政治情勢變化等，仍增添總體 經濟
不確定性，惟隨人工智慧應用 持續深化，國際雲端服務業者 及各國

## Document 094: 261010_ml_kinsus

Source PDF: all report pdf/261010_ml_kinsus.pdf

### Page 1

Kinsus

Solid revenue growth and margin 
improvement into 2027; Buy

Reiterate Rating: BUY | PO: 1,450 TWD | Price: 1,075 TWD

Raise PO along with hike in estimates  
We raise 2026/27/28E EPS by 4%/11%/14% after incorporating (see reports) 1) 
stronger-than-expected August numbers; and 2) strengthening growth outlook of server 
CPU (central processing unit) which is also shared by NYPCB. Our PO goes to NT$1,450 
(was NT$1,250) same based on 2H27-1H28E P/E. Reiterate Buy on Kinsus given its 
growing exposure to AI (artificial intelligence) server market via high-end ABF substrate, 
which in turn bolstering its margin profile in longer-term.  
Gross margin improvement carries into 2027 
We expect Kinsus’ GM (gross margin) to reach 27.8% in 3Q26, then followed by further 
improvement toward 29.3% along with 10% revenue QoQ in 4Q26, which will be fueled 
by continuous price hike and mix improvement. For 2027, expect the company’s GM to 
reach 32.5% driven by ongoing improvements in product mix and utilization.  
Sustained growth from AI CPU and optical module 
For AI and adjacent applications, we expect at least 15-20% revenue from substrate for 
Vera CPU in 2027, and AI-related application will account for 50% of the firm’s revenue 
in ABF (Ajinomoto build-up film) business in 2028. As for BT (bismaleimide triazine) 
substrate, growth in near/mid-term will be underpinned by the demand from DSP (digital 
signal processor) and transceiver (light engine) for pluggable optical module.   
Land acquisition for further capacity expansion  
Kinsus announced that it spent NT$6bn for acquiring land in Pingzhen, which will be 
used for K7 site expansion with capacity coming on stream in 2030 at earliest, following 
the ongoing expansion in K6 for ABF (Ajinomoto build-up film) substrate (reaching 80mn 
unit capacity/month in 2029-30).

Key Metrics      
* For full definitions of iQmethod SM measures, see page 7.

Company Sector 
Semiconductors

Company Description 
Kinsus, founded in 2000, is headquartered in Taiwan and 
has production facilities in Taiwan (Taoyuan) and in China 
(Suzhou). The main business is IC substrate manufacturing, 
with a wide range of product offerings including SiP, FPGA, 
FCCSP, CSP, RF modules and FCBGA. Its clients include 
Qualcomm, AMD, NVIDIA, Broadcom, Xilinx, Altera, TI, and 
MTK etc. The main competitors include Unimicron (3037 
TT), NYPCB (8046 TT), Ibiden (4062 JP), Shinko (6967 JP), 
SEMCO (009150 KS) and AT&S (ATS AV), etc.

Investment Rationale 
We have an Buy rating on Kinsus, in view of its growing 
traction to AI server market from 2026 mainly via offering 
the ABF substrate for server CPU. For other businesses, we 
also recognize the solid growth outlook in BT substrate 
thanks to the demand from memory chips, while the 
growth in contact lens business should remain stable.

### Page 5

Price objective basis & risk 
Kinsus (KNSUF) 
We set our PO at NT$1,450 based on 35x 2H27-1H28E P/E, which is at mid-to-high-end 
level in its historical trading range (8-40x) since 2013. In our view, the multiple is backed 
by ROE and operating margin recovery to 36% and 25% in 2027-28E (vs 8% and 10% in 
2025-26E).

Upside risks to our PO are (1) tighter ABF supply, (2) increasing adoption of SiP and AiP, 
(3) lower competition pressure and (4) slow development of alternative technology.

Downside risks are (1) greater ABF expansion from peers, (2) muted SiP/AiP adoption, (3) 
more severe competition, and (4) unfavorable technology development.

Amortization

Cash Realization Ratio Cash Flow From Operations Net Income 
Asset Replacement Ratio Capex Depreciation 
Tax Rate Tax Charge Pre-Tax Income 
Net Debt-To-Equity Ratio Net Debt = Total Debt − Cash & Equivalents Total Equity

Taiwan Electronics & Semiconductors
12 October 2026 Citi Research
2
PCB and substrate- 3Q26 was a strong quarter for substrate and CCL sectors, with
demand continuously piling up. Unimicron posted +23% QoQ sales growth in 3Q26,
beating Citi/consensus forecasts by 4%/6%. We expect 4Q26E ABF sales
momentum to be even stronger vs 3Q considering all the AI chips ramping up
together. CCL names also saw strong momentum across the board thanks to
enhanced product mix and benefit from price hike in 2Q26. We expect CCL sector to

see another round of price hikes from Nov onwards. For PCB, GCE’s Sept sales were
impacted by flooding in Thailand, which led to logistics disruption, while Tripod
posted record monthly sales backed by memory demand and early pull-in before
China Golden Week. With TPCA Show coming next week, we think
substrate/CCL/PCB sectors might see some event catalysts in the near term.
However, from a fundamental perspective, we still prefer substrate
(Unimicron>NYPCB>Kinsus) > CCL (EMC> TUC) > PCB.

PC/NB Supply chain - For NB ODMs, overall NB shipments see MoM increase in Sep
(+32% MoM) due to the quarter end inventory build; combined 3Q NB shipments
were down 17% QoQ due to  the early pull-in in 1H. We forecast 4Q26E NB shipments
to further decline -7% QoQ/-28% YoY given the muted demand on rising retails
prices and the increasing constraint in components procurement, While the price
hike effect in the NB products should partially offset shipment decline, we expect

increasing margins dilution impact to the NB ODM supply chains.
For lens components - Largan reported relatively muted sales results in Sep at
NT$5,779m (+15% MoM/-7% YoY); combined 3Q revenues are below market
estimates while Genius’ 3Q revenues were slightly better than expectation. Despite
the near-term sales performance, we see more upside opportunity coming from
their new development in optical business in the longer term. We expect Genius’
project with a leading US company on optical connector and ferrule to start

shipment in 1H27. For Largan, we expect it to benefit most from FA/FAU for CPO
market. We see its current engagement with the leading US client progressing
smoothly, as evidenced by its continued new land expansion for future capacity
build-out (see our recent report).
Thermal – Sep-26 sales as reported by thermal players (AVC & Auras) were up from
Aug at +6% MoM/+42% YoY. While AVC delivered another record month thanks to
solid ASIC demand and initial Vera Rubin shipments, Auras experienced MoM

decline due to client deferring pull-ins and Thailand flooding. Going forward, we
preliminarily expect both companies to see sequential revenue growth in 4Q26E,
driven by the AWS Trainium transition from air to liquid cooling, ramp-up of the TPU
and Vera Rubin projects.
Power – Delta reported MoM revenue recovery in Sep-26 (+1% MoM/+14% YoY), with
power electronics down 4% MoM. 3Q26 revenues were up 8% QoQ but below our
and Street’s expectation. We expect Delta to regain momentum with double-digit

QoQ growth in 4Q26E as ±400V enters the initial transition period, while in the long
term, HVDC is still progressing gradually while Delta maintains its technology
leadership. For BBU, we see Sep sales showing mixed momentum yet resilient BBU
demand remains intact. Dynapack’s Sep-26 sales declined 34% y/y and 54% m/m
to NT$776mn, reflecting 1) weaker PC shipments amid quarter-end inventory
adjustments and 2) deferred shipments of one BBU product, broadly consistent with

the near-term weakness flagged in our Aug-26 sales note. We expect a meaningful
sales rebound from Oct helped by shipments of new higher-power BBU products.
Meanwhile, AES maintained solid momentum with Sep-26 sales rising 25% y/y (+1%
m/m), suggesting resilient underlying BBU demand. Similarly, STL (4931.TWO; not
covered) delivered robust 176% y/y growth, further reinforcing our positive view on
the sector. We retain our constructive stance on BBU names, supported by

sustained demand and upcoming product ramps
Passive components - Yageo reported trended up monthly sales in Sep-26, as
demand in Europe and US recovered after the summer holiday, on top of sustained
AI demand. 3Q26 revenues were slightly above cons forecasts. Current quarter top
line still beats mgmt. 's "moderate growth" guidance. We believe that growth was
again broad-based across commodity and specialty products, and driven by both
volume and price. We expect a more visible ASP uplift in 4Q26E as specialty and

contract-based business could slow vs the spot and distributor channels.

Analysts’ compensation is determined by Citi Research management and Citigroup’s senior management and is based upon
activities and services intended to benefit the investor clients of Citigroup Global Markets Inc. and its affiliates (the “Firm”).
Compensation is not linked to specific transactions or recommendations. Like all Firm employees, analysts receive
compensation that is impacted by overall Firm profitability which includes investment banking, sales and trading, and

principal trading revenues. One factor in equity research analyst compensation is arranging corporate access events between
institutional clients and the management teams of covered companies. Typically, company management is more likely to
participate when the analyst has a positive view of the company.
For financial instruments recommended in the Product in which the Firm is not a market maker, the Firm is a liquidity provider
in such financial instruments (and any underlying instruments) and may act as principal in connection with transactions in

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the

expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility
or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.
Catalyst Watch/Short Term Views (“STV”) Ratings Disclosure:

Catalyst Watch and STV Upside/Downside calls: Citi Research may also include a Catalyst Watch or STV Upside or Downside
call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in
reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is

moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day
period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds
15% against the direction of the call (unless over-ridden by the analyst). The analyst may also remove a Catalyst Watch or STV
call prior to the end of the specified period in a published research note. A Catalyst Watch/STV Upside or Downside call may be

Prepared for Kevin Lu

catalyst(s) and associated share-price movement will not materialize as expected.

Our Thoughts on USITC
Investigation No. 337
The near-term impact should be manageable, but we'll closely
monitor developments on this matter.
What's new:
• The US International Trade Commission (USITC) formally launched a patent
infringement investigation on October 9, 2026, following a complaint by
Vicor Corporation, a US-based power electronics company, against 20
entities, including Delta and others across the power supply, server assembly

and semiconductor space.
• Vicor alleges that certain imported vertical power delivery systems (used
mostly in HPC chips) have infringed US Patent 10,903,734.
• Vicor requested two types of remedy: 1) a Limited Exclusion Order (LEO);
and 2) Cease and Desist Orders (CDOs), which could restrict US imports and
sales of infringing products if granted.
Our view:
• We note that this is not the first time Delta and Vicor have had legal
disputes over patent infringement.

• We will closely monitor any developments in this matter, though we think
the impact on Delta's operations should be manageable (the products in
dispute account for high single digit % of its total revenue for 2026,  based
on our estimates).
• We remain OW.

Valuation Methodology and Risks
Delta Electronics Inc. (2308.TW)
Base case: P/E multiple of 30x based on average 2027-28 EPS.                          We believe our target multiple
is justified by the 63% earnings CAGR we estimate for 2025-28, implying a PEG of 0.5, versus
the stock's ~23x P/E and 23% earnings CAGR during 2022-25, which implied a PEG of 1.0.
Risks to Upside
n Faster-than-expected global economic growth

n Falling raw material prices
n Margin improvement
Risks to Downside
n Global economic slowdown
n Ongoing component supply tightness
n Rising raw material prices
n Margin contraction as a result of higher operating expenses or faster ASP erosion

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

## Document 116: 261013_ml_ASE

Source PDF: all report pdf/261013_ml_ASE.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-13

### Page 1

ASE Technology Holding

3Q26 sales outperformance affirms solid 
transformation progress

Reiterate Rating: BUY | PO: 900.00 TWD | Price: 762.00 TWD

3Q26 sales beat on LEAP ramp and mature ASP strength 
ASE reported 3Q26 sales +28% QoQ better than its guide, driven by stronger IC ATM 
(+20% QoQ vs. BofAe +13% QoQ) and mild upside in EMS (+42% QoQ vs. BofAe +40% 
QoQ). We believe IC ATM strength is led by modest mainstream packaging and testing 
price hike on industry supply tightness, supplemented by LEAP business ramp. EMS also 
benefited from Apple product seasonal builds and higher component cost pass through. 
With improving packaging and testing utilization and ASP and rising LEAP mix, we 
expect its IC ATM GMs to improve 300bps QoQ to 30% (vs. high-20% guide) though 
consolidated GMs (+180bps QoQ to 23%) could be partially offset by EMS outgrowth. 
AI pipeline diversification anchors capex plan in ’26-28 
We model ASE’s 4Q26 IC ATM sales +7-9% QoQ as its LEAP opportunity diversifying 
from TSMC outsourcing to direct project wins including AMD’s Venice CPU, putting sales 
on track to +42% YoY in ’26 (vs. its +35% guide). Its IC ATM GMs could further expand 
to 31% as LEAP reaching 30% of its sales (vs. 18%/22% in 1H/3Q26). While we expect 
ASE to spend US$13bn+ annual capex in ’27-28 (vs. US$10.5bn in ’26), the accelerating 
IC ATM sales (BofAe +40% CAGR from ’26-28) on advanced expansion and mature price 
uplift, along with the business mix upgrade to cloud AI/HPC projects carrying 35-45% 
GMs should offset hiking depreciation (BofAe +39% CAGR from ’26-28), lifting its IC 
ATM GM from 29% in ’26 to low 30% in ’27 and mid 30% in ’28 with operating leverage. 
Stronger earnings quality unlocks re-rating potential 
We lift our ’26/27/28E EPS to NT$20/NT$34/NT$50 on strong LEAP outlook. Keep Buy 
rating and raise POs from NT$800 (US$51) to NT$900 (US$58) still on 20x 2H27-1H28 
P/E (vs. its 5-26x range) backed by its structural business transformation with better 
sales and GMs growth profile as the industry supply further consolidates amid higher 
tech entry barrier.

Company Sector 
Semiconductors

Company Description 
Established in 1984 and headquartered in Taiwan, ASE 
Technology Holding is the no.1 outsourced assembly and 
testing (OSAT) firm globally, in terms of market share by 
revenue. ASE acquired Siliconware Precision (SPIL) in 2018, 
and had acquired USI in 2010, which became its EMS 
business segment providing synergies in system-level 
packaging. 2 shares = 1 ADR.

Investment Rationale 
We rate ASE Buy, factoring in the company's opportunity in 
advanced packaging and testing evidenced in TSMC's 
outsourcing and its own heavy capex investment. The 
strategy shift should allow the company to capture fast 
growing addressable market in high performance 
computing applications and improves its margins. ASE 
could maintain its leading position in the Outsourced 
Semiconductor Assembly and Test (OSAT) universe, owing 
to its well-established footprints and widespread service 
offerings.

### Page 5

Price objective basis & risk 
ASE Technology Holding (XSRIF / ASX) 
We value ASE Technology Holding at NT$900 per share (US$58 for ADR), based on 20x 
2H27-1H28E P/E, above its historical range of 5-26x, in view of its solidified industry 
position in AI era. We use P/E ratio to value ASE as the company has been able to 
maintain healthy profitability through the cycles in the past 20 years due to 
consolidating industry landscape supporting a more benign supply/demand and keeping 
pricing and margins less cyclical.

Downside risks to our PO are 1) share loss and/or ASP erosion owing to Chinese players' 
cannibalization and/or ASE's worsening execution, 2) weakening end demand due to 
uncontrollable matters, such as macro halt and/or geopolitical tensions, 3) regulatory 
issues that could hinder ASE from generating synergies and benefit its competitors.

向晶圓代工 、IC 設計 、OSAT 送樣 ，1H27 有望進入量產 ；Insertion 
2 設備預計 2H26 會有較明顯的結果。

供貨比重大多為 80% ，但之前因為產能供不應求，有外溢到第
二供應商，現在有積極擴產。

載板及 PCB 。旺矽表示，探針及載板均已全數自製， PCB 預計
2027 年開始量產，目標將 PCB 的自製率提升至 50% 。

  評等  定義  
  強力買進 (Strong Buy)  首次評等潛在上漲空間 ≥ 35%  
  買進 (Buy)  15% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <35%  
  區間操作 (Trading Buy) 5% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <15%

中立 (Neutral)  
無法由基本面給予投資評等  
預期近期股價將處於盤整  
建議降低持股

【投資評等調整機制】  
  投資評等係「首次」給予特定個股投資評等時，其前一日收盤價相較 3 個月
目標價之潛在上漲空間計算而得。個股投資評等分為四個等級，定義如上。
爾後的投資評等係依循「首次評等」，直到停止推薦。   
「強力買進」、「買進」及「區間操作」均有 upside 目標價。差別僅在於，
首次評等時潛在報酬率不同。「中立」則無目標價。  
一旦我們給予特定個股「強力買進」 、 「買進」或「區間操作」之投資評等，
就是責任的開始，爾後將透過各式研究報告作定期性、持續性追蹤基本面及
股價變化，直到停止推薦。  
停止推薦情境：  
1.  達目標價。  
2.  雖未達原訂目標價，但檢視基本面、訊息面、籌碼面等多方訊息，研
判股價上漲空間已然有限，將適時出具降評報告。  
3.  推薦後股價不漲反跌。

【免責聲明】  
  本研究報告僅提供予特定人之客戶作為參考資料「非經同意不得轉載」 。我
們並不確保此資訊的完整性與正確性，投資人應了解，報告中有關未來預測
之陳述可能不會實現，因而不應被依賴。而且此報告並非根據特定投資目的
或依預定對象之財務狀況所撰寫出來的，因此，此研究報告的目的，既非對
投資人於買賣證券、選擇權、期貨或其他證券相關之衍生性商品提供詢價服
務，亦非作為進行交易的要約。投資人應注意到相關證券之價值及收益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

元。 1H26 毛利率  33.13% EPS 25.77 元。  
 隨著 VR200 與 ASIC 伺服器專案陸續放量 ， 伺服器產品比重提
升，再加上新產能第四季陸續開出，公司營收、毛利將維持成
長動能。  
 2027 年受惠於 Vera Rubin 平台與 ASIC 進入放量階段，下一
世代 Vera Rubin Ultra 機櫃，預期將導入更高規格 ZQD ，將
有望帶動單櫃快接頭產值提升。

一. 2Q26 毛利率達  34.05% ，EPS 12.39 元 
富世達 2Q26 營收 37.4 億， QoQ -10.6% ，YoY +42.8% ，營收較
前一季下滑主要由於手機、伺服器產品處於新舊產品轉換期影
響。而獲利方面， 2Q26 營業毛利 13.5 億， QoQ -5.8% ，YoY 
+101.6% ，毛利率 34.05% ，QoQ +1.74ppts ，YoY +9.98ppts ，
2Q26 營業利益 10.4 億，QoQ -6.9% ，YoY +109.9% ，公司表示，
受惠於製程持續優化、伺服器產品佔比提升至五成以上，帶動毛
利率提升 。2Q26 業外淨利 0.3 億 ， 稅後淨利8.49 億，QoQ -7.4% ，
YoY +157.3% ，單季稅後 EPS 12.39 元。

二. 預估 2026 年稅後 EPS 63.08  
公司公告 9 月營收 16.13 億， MoM +5.6% ，YoY +32.2% ，3Q26
營收 45.02 億，QoQ +36.1% ，YoY +79.3% ，今年累計營收 124.3
億， YoY +47.3% 。展望 2H26 ，公司預期隨著 VR200 與 ASIC 伺
服器專案陸續放量，水冷快接頭與滑軌的出貨比重將顯著提升，
再加上新產能第四季將陸續開出，公司營收將維持成長動能。而
摺疊手機部分，受惠於客戶發布新款產品，亦將推升摺疊手機業
務回溫。此外，由於高毛利伺服器產品比重提升，公司預期毛利
率將持續攀升，且營業費用率亦將隨營收規模擴大而逐漸下降。
預估 2026 全年營收 181.8 億元， YoY +46.5% ，毛利率 35.2% ，
營利率 29.4% ，稅後淨利 43.2 億，全年稅後 EPS 63.08 元。

三. 預估 2027 年稅後 EPS 112.24 元 
展望 2027 年，受惠於 Vera Rubin 平台與 ASIC 進入主要放量階
段， QD 應用亦從 Switch Tray 延伸至 Compute Tray 及 Rack 
Manifold ， 將帶動機櫃快接頭用量提升 。 此外 ， 由於晶片功耗提升 、
投資評等：買進

冷卻液流量需求增加， NVIDIA 下一世代的 Vera Rubin Ultra 機
櫃，預期將導入更高規格 ZQD ，單價預估將比現行的 MQD/UQD

四. 投資建議為「買進」，目標價 2920 元

需求，公司快接頭 (QD) 產品成長動能明確，公司受惠於各項專案
的陸續發酵，將顯著推升公司伺服器產品的營收貢獻，亦有望帶
動公司營收與獲利維持成長動能。目前股價交易於 2027 年 EPS
約 21~22 倍本益比，評價仍具上修空間，給於買進投資建議，目
標價 2920 元(26x 2027 EPS) 。

貳、產 業 及 營 運 概 況

一.公司簡介  
富世達 (FOSITEK) 成立於 2001 年，為奇鋐科技 (3017) 旗下子公
司，主要從事電子類、電腦、資訊通信產業等 3C 電子產品轉軸組
件之研發、製造及銷售。公司產品主要應用於電腦、電子、光學、
家電、資訊、通信、醫療設備等產品之連結軸，如商用 NB 轉軸、
工業用 NB 轉軸 、 軍事用NB 轉軸 、LCD Monitor 轉軸 、PDA 轉軸、
LCD-TV 轉軸、手機轉軸、螢幕懸臂支架、醫療懸臂支架等。公司
初期與京東方、華為三方合作生產研發摺疊手機，為華為摺疊手
機的主要開案合作廠商。近年積極投入水冷快接頭模組量產，並
也陸續送給鴻海、廣達等組裝廠進行測試，同時也通過數家水冷
板供應商認證，已成功打入輝達 GB200 與 GB300 供應鏈出貨水
冷快接頭 (QD/UQD) 與滑軌， 2025 年折疊手機佔公司整體營收的
56.4%， 筆記型電腦佔6.4%， 伺服器產品佔36.3%， 其他佔0.9%。
公司總部位於台北新莊新北產業園區，並於大陸深圳及越南設有
生產據點。公司 AI 伺服器與水冷零件主要客戶為 NVIDIA 及 CSP
大廠；手機主要客戶包含華為、 Motorola ，Google 等；筆電客戶
包含 Dell 、HP、聯想、華為。而公司主要競爭對手則包含新日興
(3376) 、兆利科技 (3548) 等。

圖一、富世達 1H26 產品營收佔比

資料來源：富世達

二.產業趨勢概況  
1. 四大 CSP 上修今年資本支出達 7,450 億美元 
在生成式 AI 商機快速擴張帶動下，全球雲端基礎設施投資進入新
一輪高峰。亞馬遜、 Alphabet 和微軟認為其在先前的鉅額投資已
帶動營收明顯成長 ， 同時預估未來AI 潛在商機仍持續擴大 。 其中 ，
亞馬遜、 Alphabet 和 Meta 已陸續上調 2026 全年投資金額，且四
大雲端服務供應商 (CSP) 總資本支出將上升至 7,450 億美元新高
規模。亞馬遜、微軟、 Alphabet 與 Meta 2026 年第 1、2 季分別
為 1,306 億、1,701 億美元，同時亞馬遜、 Alphabet 和 Meta 再上
修 2026 全年資本支出金額， 2026 年資本支出預估將達 7,450 億
美元。而根據 TrendForce 最新產業研究，全球主要雲端服務供應
商(CSP) 為加速建置 AI 基礎設施，資本支出預估於 2026 年將年
增 98%，2027 年再成長 50% 至 1.3 兆美元左右，年增率收斂的主
要原因，除了反映高基期因素之外，部分可歸咎為鉅額資本支出
對四大 CSP 財務衝擊日益擴大。然而，在 AI Inference 、Agentic 
AI、自研 ASIC 和新一代 AI 模型持續推升算力需求的情況下，預
期整體 CSP 投資金額將續創新高紀錄，產業成長有望從原先以
GPU 擴張為主，轉為包含 AI server 、液冷散熱、先進封裝、高速
互聯、電源和記憶體等基礎設施全面升級的態勢。

資料來源： TrendForce 、宏遠投顧整理

2. 2026 年全球 AI 伺服器出貨量預計年增 31% 
根據 TrendForce 最新 AI server 產業研究，近期超大型 CSP 、
Tier-2 資料中心業者對 NVIDIA GB/VR 等整櫃式 AI server 方案的
採購意願明顯提高，且 Google 、AWS 新一代 ASIC 平台於 2026
下半年陸續放量，加上中系業者擴大採用本地 AI 方案以滿足雲端
大型語言模型 (LLM) AI 需求，促使 TrendForce 上調 2026 年 AI 
server 出貨年成長幅度，從原先的 28% 更新為近 31%。北美五大
業者 2026 年的資本支出多翻倍提升，反映出大型 CSP 廠持續擴
建 AI 資料中心、 GPU 叢集、液冷散熱等新一代基礎設施，以應對
生成式 AI 及大型模型快速成長帶來的算力需求。其中， Google 
2026 至 2027 年都將側重自家 TPU 晶片發展， 2027 年出貨量有
望繼續高速成長。 AWS 2026 年以 NVIDIA GB300 作為 GPU AI 
server 主力，自研 ASIC 也穩定出貨，預期至 2027 年量能將再擴
大。 Meta 2026 年主要採用 NVIDIA GB/VR 、AMD Helios 整櫃式
方案，自研 ASIC 布局預計至 2027 年將明顯加速，為該公司長期
降低 AI 基礎設施成本、提升推論效率的重要策略。

3.液冷散熱成高階 AI 基礎設施標配，估 2026 年滲透率達 53% 
根據 TrendForce 最新研究顯示，隨著 AI 基礎設施建置浪潮及高
階晶片運算功率不斷攀升，單晶片熱設計功耗已超越 1kW ，使液
冷散熱技術正式成為高階 AI 基礎設施的標準配置。在 NVIDIA 、
AMD 與 Google 等巨頭的高階晶片與整櫃式方案強力推動下，預
估 2026 年液冷散熱於 AI 晶片的滲透率將從 2025 年的 33% 大幅
躍升至 53%，並在 2027 年進一步逼近 60%。其中， Google 因積
極導入自研 TPU 並率先全面推廣整櫃式液冷設計，成為主要推手
之一。此外，隨著 NVIDIA Vera Rubin 平台全面導入全液冷架構，
水冷板、分歧管及冷卻分配系統等關鍵零組件需求隨之大增，帶

4. 蘋果入局帶動摺疊機高成長，估今年全球摺疊機出貨年增 24% 
根據 Counterpoint Research 報告 ， 全球摺疊智慧型手機累計出貨
量預計將於 2026 年底突破 1 億支大關，達成自 2019 年首度問世
以來的重大里程碑。隨著消費者對大尺寸書本式摺疊機的需求攀
升，加上蘋果 (Apple) 推出首款摺疊 iPhone 的帶動，預估 2026 年
全球摺疊機出貨量將年增 24% ，2027 年出貨預估年增 37% 。而
根據 Counterpoint Research 預估 ， 三星(Samsung) 憑藉 Galaxy Z 
Fold8 等大尺寸書本式設計的強勁動能 ， 預計在2026 年將以  38%
的市占率穩居全球龍頭寶座；而蘋果 (Apple) 推出的首款摺疊
iPhone ，雖預期在初期會面臨產能限制，但仍將為高階市場帶來
立即的衝擊，預估 2026 年就能迅速搶下 25% 的市占率空降全球
第二；而華為 (Huawei) 則預估以 22% 的市占率位居第三，主要由
於其在中國市場依然保持強勢的主導地位，並受到  Pura X Max 
等產品的推動。

叁、業 績 及 獲 利 預 估

一. 預估 2026 年稅後 EPS 63.08  
公司公告 9 月營收 16.13 億， MoM +5.6% ，YoY +32.2% ，3Q26
營收 45.02 億，QoQ +36.1% ，YoY +79.3% ，今年累計營收 124.3
億， YoY +47.3% 。展望 2H26 ，公司預期隨著 VR200 與 ASIC 伺
服器專案陸續放量，水冷快接頭與滑軌的出貨比重將顯著提升，
再加上新產能第四季將陸續開出，公司營收將維持成長動能。而
摺疊手機部分，受惠於客戶發布新款產品，亦將推升摺疊手機業
務回溫。此外，由於高毛利伺服器產品比重提升，公司預期毛利
率將持續攀升，且營業費用率亦將隨營收規模擴大而逐漸下降。
預估 2026 全年營收 181.8 億元， YoY +46.5% ，毛利率 35.2% ，
營利率 29.4% ，稅後淨利 43.2 億，全年稅後 EPS 63.08 元。

二. 預估 2027 年稅後 EPS 112.24 元 
展望 2027 年，受惠於 Vera Rubin 平台與 ASIC 進入主要放量階
段， QD 應用亦從 Switch Tray 延伸至 Compute Tray 及 Rack 
Manifold ， 將帶動機櫃快接頭用量提升 。 此外 ， 由於晶片功耗提升 、
冷卻液流量需求增加， NVIDIA 下一世代的 Vera Rubin Ultra 機
櫃，預期將導入更高規格 ZQD ，單價預估將比現行的 MQD/UQD
顯著提升，將有望帶動單櫃快接頭產值提升。預估 2027 全年營收
295.5 億元， YoY +46.5% ，毛利率 37.6% ，營利率 32.3% ，稅後
淨利 76.9 億，全年稅後 EPS 112.24 元。

### Page 1

매수 (유지)  
여전히 가격 상승 구간 
SK하이닉스의 26년 3분기 매출액은 95.8조원, 영업이익 75.1조원으로 예상한다. 
이전 전망 대비 감소한 수준이다. 원/달러 환율 영향이다. 영업이익률은 78.4%로 전 
분기 대비 2.1%p 상승할 전망이다. DRAM 매출액은 73.1조원, 영업이익 
59.2조원으로 추정한다. DRAM ASP는 이전 전망 대비 상향 조정했다. 일부 제품 
가격 인상을 반영한 영향이다. Bit Growth는 이전 전망 수준을 유지했다. ASP 
상향이 수익성 개선에 미친 영향은 크지 않을 전망이다. NAND 매출액은 22.4조원, 
영업이익은 15.9조원으로 예상한다. 
Global Top 수준 주주환원 규모/자사주 살 게 더 있다 
SK하이닉스는 2026년 8월 19일 주주환원에 대한 새로운 정책을 제시했다. FCF에서 
CAPEX를 제외한 금액의 50% 이상을 주주환원에 사용한다는게 핵심이다. 2026년 
기준으로 최소 90조원 수준일 것으로 추정한다. 자사주 중심으로 주주환원을 계획 
중이고 이미 집행 중인 40조원 이외에 4분기 중에 추가로 자사주 매입이 진행될 
것으로 예상한다. 배당도 일부 집행이 되겠지만 자사주가 중심이 될 전망이다. 
흔들리지 말아야 할 이유는 많다 
SK하이닉스에 대한 투자의견은 매수를 유지한다. AI 모델의 성장으로 메모리에 
대한 수요가 지속적으로 높아지고 있고, 이에 비해 공급 증가는 제한적인 상황이 
상당기간 지속될 것으로 전망하기 때문이다. 이로 인해 현재 수준의 DRAM, NAND 
가격에 변동성은 낮고, 높은 수익성이 유지되면서 안정적 현금흐름 창출이 가능할 
것으로 전망한다. 현재 주가는 지나치게 저평가되어 있는 국면에 있다고 판단한다. 
목표주가는 12개월 예상 EPS 446,092원에 PER 9 배를 적용한 4,000,000원을 
유지한다.

Compliance Notice 
동 자료에 게재된 내용들은 외부의 압력이나 부당한 간섭없이 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하여 작성되었음을 확인합니다. 
동 자료는 기관투자가 또는 제3자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다. 
동 자료는 조사분석자료 작성에 참여한 외부인(계열회사 및 그 임직원등)이 없습니다. 
조사분석 담당자 및 배우자는 해당종목과 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다. 
동 자료에 언급된 종목의 지분율 1%이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다. 
당사는 상기 명시한 사항 외 고지해야 하는 특별한 이해관계가 없습니다.

종목명 담당자 담당자(배우자) 보유여부 1%이상 보유여부 유가증권 발행관련 계열사 관계여부 공개매수 사무취급 IPO 회사채 지급보증 중대한 이해관계 M&A 관련 수량 취득가 취득일 
해당 사항 없음

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
TODAY’S INSIGHTS
Financial Markets
Markets were pulled between renewed optimism around AI 
and a broader rise in geopolitical and fiscal risks. A rebound in 
chipmakers lifted US equities as investors looked to another 
major round of earnings for reassurance that the AI investment 
boom can continue to deliver. Elsewhere, oil remained near 
five-month highs as escalating Middle East tensions kept 
supply risks elevated, while mass protests across France 
over cost-of-living and dissatisfaction around education 
raised concerns that governments could be pushed towards 
additional spending, adding to already stretched debt burdens.
• Tech stocks led the rebound in US equities overnight. The 
Nasdaq outperformed, rising 1.1%, while the S&P 500 gained 
0.6% while the Dow Jones closed 0.1% lower. Attention now 
turns to another busy week of corporate earnings, with several 
major companies including SpaceX, Tesla and Meta Platforms 
due. European equities moved in the opposite direction, 
with protests across several countries weighing on regional 
sentiment. The Euro Stoxx 50 fell 1.0% and the FTSE 100 
declined 1.1%.
• Asian markets were mixed. Japan’s Nikkei 225 finished 0.6% 
lower, recovering from an earlier fall of as much as 2.3%, 
as concerns around the funding demands of AI investment 
and rising interest rates remained in focus ahead of earnings 
season. Korean equities rebounded strongly, with the Kospi 
gaining 1.8%, supported by comments from government 
officials and a recovery in Samsung. Chinese equities were 
supported by a stronger-than-expected GDP result, with the 
CSI 300 gaining 0.1%. In Australia, the ASX 200 rose 0.4%. 
Hong Kong markets were closed for a holiday.
• Treasuries fell as a rebound in US technology shares sapped 
haven demand for US government debt, lifting yields across 
maturities. The 2-year and 10-year rose 6bps and 7bps 
respectively. The Federal Reserve remains in a communication 
blackout period until it meets later this month. Futures 
markets currently price in a 17% chance of a rate hike. Rising 
yields across European government bond markets added to 
the pressure on Treasuries. France led the sell-off, with the 
10-year OAT yield climbing 13bps, while 10-year Bund and 
gilt yields rose 3bps and 6bps respectively.
• Australian government bond yields were little changed across 
the 3-year and 10-year maturities. In a fireside chat, RBA 
Assistant Governor (Financial Markets) Christopher Kent said 
there was reasonable evidence that the neutral rate was rising 
and questioned whether monetary policy was sufficiently 
restrictive. On the labour market, Kent cautioned against 
placing too much weight on individual monthly employment 
readings, but acknowledged that conditions had become less 
tight and were loosening faster than the RBA had forecast. 
Markets currently price around a 23% chance of a rate hike at 
the RBA’s November meeting.
• The US dollar index rose 0.1%. The AUD gained against the 
USD for a second consecutive session, up 0.5% to 0.6979. 
Other USD crosses were mixed with the yen up 0.3%, with the 
euro and pound losing 0.2% against the greenback.
• Brent settled slightly lower, falling 0.4% to US109.47/bbl but 
recovered from a low of US$106.82/bbl. Meanwhile, WTI rose 
2.3%, closing at US$103.40/bbl. Other commodities were 
mixed, with gold futures down 0.6%, copper losing 0.5%, 
while iron ore rose 0.4%.
International Data
The US NAHB housing market index declined from 32 to 
29 in October, a new low since the GFC. Cost-of-living and 
serviceability continue to weigh heavily on sales activity and 
sentiment towards housing.

Yesterday, China GDP surprised to the upside in Q3 2026, 
quarterly growth accelerating from 0.9% in Q2 to 1.2% in Q3. 
Annual growth lifted from 4.3% to 4.5% as a result, keeping 
authorities on track to achieve their 4.5-5.0%ytd ambition 
for 2026. While annual retail sales growth accelerated from 
0.4% to 1.1% in September, the Q3 year-to-date growth 
pulse of 1.1% is just a quarter of the gain reported a year ago. 
Total fixed asset investment has been an outright negative for 
annual GDP growth since April, but a recovery is not beyond 
either the public or private sector’s capacity. Rather, across the 
economy, entities are waiting for direction and incentive from 
the Central Government. This is likely to come by early-2027. 
Helpfully, industrial production continues to grow at a robust 
pace, 5.3%ytd, borrowing rates for businesses and households 
are low, and authorities are beginning to provide banks with 
additional lending capacity (see here for more information).

## Document 352: Q3 China GDP above expectations, but downside risks remain (2026-10-20)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Q3 China GDP above expectations, but downside risks remain (2026-10-20).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#china #asia #aud
Q3 China GDP above expectations, but downside risks remain
By Elliot Clarke
Head of International Economics, Westpac
16:03 October 19 2026 Share
Stimulus is required if success in 2026 is to be sustained through 2027 and 2028.
China GDP surprised to the upside in Q3 2026, quarterly growth accelerating from 0.9% in Q2 to 1.2%
in Q3. Annual growth lifted from 4.3% to 4.5% as a result; though, on a year-to-date basis, growth
edged down 0.1ppts to 4.6%. The Q3 result keeps authorities on track to achieve their 4.5-5.0%ytd

ambition for 2026, and also provides a little more time to accelerate domestic demand into 2027.

### Page 2

Broad-based stimulus is required because net exports’ contribution to growth will likely abate in coming
quarters now that the trade surplus is back at record highs, and as many Chinese firms are
increasingly splitting their focus between expanding domestic capacity and offshore production
networks.

While annual retail sales growth accelerated from 0.4% to 1.1% in September, the Q3 year-to-date
growth pulse of 1.1% is just a quarter of the gain reported a year ago. Automobiles, construction
materials and jewellery have been particular heavy detractors over the past year, but most of the other
sub-categories also lack momentum. As we detailed in our September Market Outlook, this is not
because households are cash constrained, but rather as sentiment is extremely weak and the outlook
for employment and wealth highly uncertain. Key for wealth is that house prices for new and
established homes continue to decline month-on-month (respectively 0.1% and 0.3% in September)
five years into the downturn. Property investment’s ongoing decline (-20.6%ytd) suggests this
persistent weakness in sales and prices is demand driven.

While total fixed asset investment has been an outright negative for annual GDP growth since April, a
recovery is not beyond either the public or private sector’s capacity. Rather, across the economy,
entities are waiting for direction and incentive from the Central Government. This is likely to come by
early-2027 to offset the loss of support from trade, and as downside risks to global growth become
more apparent. Helpfully, industrial production continues to grow at a robust pace, 5.3%ytd, borrowing
rates for businesses and households are low, and authorities are beginning to provide banks with
additional lending capacity. For larger businesses, anecdotes suggest officials are also encouraging
major institutional investors to increase their structural allocation to domestic equities, building lasting 
appetite for new equity issuance, which in turn will fund future waves of real economic capacity and
income generation.

We remain comfortable with the view that Chinese GDP growth will hold at or slightly above 4.5%
through 2026–2028. But, the longer the Central Government waits to trigger new real investment
across both the private and public sectors, the higher the likelihood of poor sentiment becoming
entrenched and downside risks crystallising. If authorities wait until after net exports’ contribution has
faded, even holding annual aggregate growth above 4.0% could become a significant challenge. For
this reason, we expect material stimulus initiatives to be announced in the next 3 to 6 months to reset
the property sector and turn the business investment trend.

Get the latest reports straight to your inbox.

### Page 2

The Week Ahead October 2, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         2

CENTRAL BANK MEETINGS

India: RBI monetary policy committee 
meeting (7-Oct)

The macro environment has undergone 
significant changes since the RBI 
monetary policy committee’s (MPC) 
August policy meeting, with global oil 
prices rising past $100bl, and US Fed 
kickstarting their rate hike cycle. 
Domestically, core and headline 
continued to rise, laying the ground for 
inflation to quicken beyond 5.8 -6.0% in 
the second half as price risks broaden. 
Strong 1QFY27 GDP growth meanwhile 
pointed to the economy’s relative 
resilience to external shocks.

Besides fuel feeding into food costs, 
weather has also proved to be disruptive, 
with indications that the El Niño 
phenomenon could extend into 2027. 
International food benchmarks, including 
the UN Food Price index and the 
Bloomberg spot agricultural indice s, 
continue to climb. India’s food inflation 
has become more broad -based, shifting 
beyond vegetables and pulses to sugar, 
milk, protein groups, and edible oils. Sub-
par weather has contributed to this 
upmove, besides idiosyncratic factors 
like swing in production trends (cobweb), 
diversion for fuel usage etc.

After two years of normal monsoon, 
cumulative southwest rainfall this year is 
set to end with rainfall around 12 -13% 
below the long -term average, the most 
since 2015 and resulting in a slower 
build-up in reservoir levels.  
Global benchmark oil price gauge has 
averaged ~$93pb in FY27 year -to-date, 
up 36% yoy  vs FY26. Retail pump fuel 
prices were adjusted in quick succession 
in May26 and have been left unchanged 
since then, while other fuel products 
including CNG, aviation fuel, LPG etc 
have continued to climb. Add to this, 
other pockets like telecom, restaura nts, 
business services, transport fares etc 
have been inching up, reflecting early 
signs of broadening in price pressures (on 
sequential basis). A gradual broadening 
of price pressures is likely to keep 
headline inflation high and narrow the 
real rate buffer significantly.

Authorities are also conducting liquidity 
absorption measures through market 
operations like VRRR, open market 
operations and sell-buy FX swaps. This is 
likely to align the overnight call money 
rates to the benchmark repo rate, after 
having fallen sharply d ue to the surplus 
in the banking system.  Liquidity and core 
inflation have historically displayed a 
modest positive relationship, as per our 
study. As such, the RBI's inflation 
mandate has likely reinforced the push to 
drain excess liquidity.

We expect the RBI MPC to raise the repo 
rate by 25bp to 5.5% , citing pipeline 
inflationary pressures. Stance could be 
changed to withdrawal of 
accommodation to signal hawkish intent. 
We expect one more hike within 4Q26, 
though the odds of additional hikes have 
risen as oil prices stay stubbornly high 
and weather remains unfavourable.

### Page 3

The Week Ahead October 2, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         3

FORTHCOMING DATA 
RELEASES

Malaysia

We expect industrial production to grow 
by 5.8% yoy in August from 4.7% yoy in 
July. Strength in  export-oriented 
manufacturing is likely to persist , 
supported by sustained demand for 
artificial intelligence-related electronics 
goods, while mining output may have 
recovered from its contraction in July.

Taiwan

September trade and inflation data are 
forthcoming. Export growth is expected 
to sustain the 40% pace seen in the 
previous month. The global AI boom is 
facing rising headwinds from 
synchronised rate hikes by G3 central 
banks, higher bond yields, and rising  
financing costs. That said, there are so 
far no clear signs of a significant 
slowdown, based on the high -frequency 
indicators we track across AI demand, 
investment, and supply.

CPI is expected to rebound to 2.4% YoY 
in September, up from 2.0% in August. 
The earlier arrival of the Mid -Autumn 
Festival this year may have boosted food 
and services prices on a YoY basis. The 
rebound in global energy prices should 
also have lifted tran sport costs, 
including gasoline prices and airfares. 
While electricity tariffs will remain 
frozen, the December review implies a 
possibility of an electricity price hike 
after the local elections.

Given still -strong economic growth, 
persistent inflation, and the potential for 
inflation expectations to rise, we 
maintain our view that the central bank 
will raise the policy rate by a modest 
12.5bps to 2.125% in December.

Thailand

We expect Thailand’s headline inflation 
to accelerate to 3.2% yoy in September 
from 2.5% yoy  in August, slightly 
exceeding the central bank’s 1 -3% 
inflation target. Faster increases in 
energy and food prices likely pushed 
overall inflation higher, despite an 
easing in electricity tariffs starting 
September. We expect the Bank of 
Thailand (BOT), w hich views the rise in 
inflation as largely supply -driven, to 
keep its policy rate stable at 1.00% for 
the remainder of 2026, aiming to support 
an economy with low and uneven growth.

## Document 378: The Wrap on Asia_ Asia’s goods disinflation over_ (2026-10-20)

Source PDF: all report pdf/The Wrap on Asia_ Asia’s goods disinflation over_ (2026-10-20).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
1
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WRAP ON ASIA
Mapping out Asia ...
Illiana Jain
Economist, Westpac Group
P: +61 403  908 032
E: illiana.jain@westpac.com.au
• For two decades, lower labour costs across Asia helped
suppress global goods inflation.
• Much of that disinflationary pulse originated in North Asia,

where production was increasingly offshored to lower-cost
labour markets.
• Today, ageing populations and rising incomes are lifting
labour costs across much of the region.
• While production continues to migrate towards South
and Southeast Asia, the next phase of manufacturing is
increasingly driven by capital, technology and power rather
than low-cost labour.
• As a result, labour costs are unlikely to provide the same
disinflationary impulse that supported goods prices over the

previous decade.
• Technology has historically provided a source of disinflation
as production costs fell and competition intensified.
• Asia sits at the centre of the AI supply chain through firms
such as TSMC, Samsung and SK Hynix, which produce
critical semiconductors and memory chips used in AI
infrastructure.
• Rising costs for electricity and critical minerals are increasing
production costs across the sector.
• Strong demand has allowed firms to pass these costs on

while maintaining healthy profitability. SK Hynix’s net profit
rose from ₩19.8tn to ₩42.9tn (+117%yr).
• Firms are also competing on performance and technological
capability rather than price, allowing technology-related
inflation to emerge for the first time in years.
• Many Asian economies remain reliant on imported energy,
leaving producers exposed to higher oil and LNG costs.
• Semiconductor manufacturing and the broader AI
infrastructure build-out are highly energy intensive.

Expansions by TSMC, Samsung and SK Hynix are increasing
electricity demand across North Asia while data-centre
investment adds a further source of demand.
• As technology firms compete with traditional manufacturers
for power, energy costs are becoming an increasingly
important part of the production process.
• Rising production costs are therefore being driven by both
higher energy prices and stronger underlying demand for
electricity.

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WRAP ON ASIA
• Chinese producer-price deflation remains evident across
parts of traditional manufacturing, particularly sectors
facing persistent overcapacity such as automobiles
• Historically, Chinese producer prices acted as the pricing
anchor for much of Asia’s manufacturing sector.

• What has changed is that parts of the region are increasingly
specialising in industries where China is not the dominant
supplier.
• Strong demand for semiconductors, memory and AI-related
goods is allowing firms in Taiwan and South Korea to set
prices according to their own market conditions despite
ongoing weakness in Chinese producer prices.
• China’s deflationary pulse remains important, but it no
longer guarantees lower prices across the rest of Asia.

• Rising costs were previously offset by production shifting to
lower-cost economies elsewhere in Asia.
• Production continues to migrate towards Vietnam, India,
Indonesia, Malaysia and Thailand, helping contain costs.
• However, these economies are unlikely to replicate the scale
of China’s integration into global manufacturing during the
2000s.
• High-value production remains concentrated in North
Asia while firms increasingly value resilience, security and

specialisation alongside cost minimisation.
• Future supply-chain shifts may therefore help limit
cost pressures, but are unlikely to generate the same
disinflationary pulse seen over the last two decades.
• For much of the past decade, falling goods prices helped
offset elevated services inflation across developed markets.
The forces that drove that disinflation are now becoming
less powerful.
• While Chinese deflation and production relocation to lower-

cost economies will continue to provide some support, they
are unlikely to fully offset these pressures.
• As a result, imported goods may provide less assistance in
bringing inflation back to target than they have in the past.
• This leaves central banks increasingly reliant on containing
domestic inflation pressures, particularly in services.
• As a result, achieving inflation targets may require a longer
period of restrictive monetary policy than has historically

been the case.
... in charts

### Page 4

## Document 380: Threats to European diesel imports_ risk of shortages ruled out for the time being, but increased risk of price rises (2026-10-01)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Threats to European diesel imports_ risk of shortages ruled out for the time being, but increased risk of price rises (2026-10-01).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Paris, 1st October 2026

EcoFlash

Threats to European diesel imports: risk of shortages ruled out for the time being, but increased 
risk of price rises

In Europe, the possibility of a US ban on diesel exports has heightened concerns about price trends for this widely used 
fuel1, which has already risen significantly since the outbreak of the conflict in Iran. It has become a major political issue 
on both sides of the Atlantic in the run-up to decisive elections.

A particularly tight diesel market due to a shortage of buffer stocks 
Following the outbreak of the conflict, diesel prices did indeed see some of the sharpest rises on the oil market due to particularly 
tight market conditions. Since the end of February, whilst the price of a barrel of crude has risen by 46%, diesel prices have soared 
by 100% in Europe and by 76% in the United States.

The factors alleviating pressure on the refined products market, and more specifically the diesel market, were more limited than in 
the case of crude oil. Shipments passing through the Strait of Hormuz (around 17% of maritime diesel trade prior to the conf lict) 
have fallen by around 75%. Added to this is the halt in Russian exports due to war damage, which is depriving the diesel market of 
around 12% of its maritime exports. Furthermore, unlike with crude oil, China has not acted as a stabilising  force in the diesel 
market. On the contrary, the energy crisis has led to a halt in Chinese exports of refined products (including diesel) to the  rest of 
Asia from April onwards. The resumption of Chinese exports over the last two months remains fragile, given, in particular, that 
diesel stocks are at their lowest level in 15 months.

The United States has eased these tensions in the diesel market by increasing its exports of refined products (up 25% on average 
in the first half of 2026 compared with the average over the last five years). This rise in exports has required high refinery utilisation 
rate (close to maximum capacity) and, to a lesser extent, a reduction in stocks (-11% compared with the average over the last five 
years as at mid -September for middle distillates 2). All these factors have contributed to tightening the US domestic market and 
pushed retail diesel prices to record levels. Road diesel prices (US average) have risen by 68% since the end of February and by 
74% year-on-year.

A structurally high level of vulnerability in Europe… 
Against this market backdrop, characterised by mounting tensions and very limited room for manoeuvre , Europe would be 
particularly hard hit by a reduction in – or even a complete halt to – US exports. According to Eurostat, imports accounted for 46% 
of European demand for diesel in the first half of 2026, with 40% of these imports coming from the United States. Europe, which

1 Diesel accounts for 17% of the primary energy mix in the EU and 7.3% in the United States. 
2 A category of refined petroleum products comprising mainly diesel and aviation fuel.

### Page 2

has lost a great deal of production capacity in recent years, is operating its refineries at near -maximum capacity. Its room for 
manoeuvre is therefore limited.

… But the risk of a shortage has been ruled out in the short term 
The risk of a shortage, however, appears limited in the short term. The situation regarding European stocks is not comfortable, but 
it is not alarming. Commercial diesel stocks in the ARA region 3 are currently at historically low levels (around 12 million barrels), 
23% below their level at the end of September 2025. As for emergency stocks (EU Emergency Stocks), these are, according to the 
European Commission, at a sufficient level. Eurostat estim ates this figure at 270 million barrels in June 2026 (stable from a year 
earlier), representing approximately 60 days’ worth of EU consumption. Europe imports around 0.9 mb/d of diesel from the United 
States, and emergency stocks would therefore cover around 60 days’ consumption at 4.5 mb/d.

The rise in prices could be significant 
Whatever the outcome of the conflict, diesel prices are likely to continue to rise in the short term. According to our base -case 
scenario, crude oil prices are expected to remain very high in Q4 2026 due to ongoing tensions in the Persian Gulf, whilst a return 
to normal levels of diesel exports from Russia and the Middle East appears unlikely by that time.

Among the factors likely to cause future tensions, the start of the maintenance season in Europe could affect production leve ls, 
whilst demand for diesel in the United States is typically high in the autumn (agricultural harvests and pre -winter storage). I n a 
scenario where tensions remain low, the diesel refining margin (crack spread) could rise by 5% in Q4 compared with its level at the 
end of September 2026, before gradually falling back during 2027. An intermediate scenario (a 10% rise in the crack spread in Q4 
2026) would result in moderate constraints on US diesel exports and a drawdown of European stocks. Finally, an adverse scenario 
(a 35% rise in the crack spread in Q4 2026) would imply severe restrictions on US diesel exports and a delayed European response.

What impact will this have on inflation in the euro area? 
According to our estimates, the rise in diesel prices would exacerbate the expected peak in inflation in the euro area without altering 
its timing. In our baseline scenario for September 2026 (see our recently published EcoPerspective), headline inflation would peak 
at 4.1% y/y in January 2027 before gradually falling back towards 2% by the end of the year. The two least adverse scenarios 
would add only a limited amount to the peak: +0.1 pp in the first scenario (+5% for the crack spread in Q4 compared with the end 
of Q3) and +0.2 pp in the intermediate scenario (+10%), with inflation reaching 4.2% and 4.3% respectively in January 2027. 
However, there is an upside risk in the second scenario linked to the uncertain extent of the spillov er effects. In both cases, the 
main impact would be felt through fuel prices (which quickly reflect rising market prices) and would fade over the course of 2027. 
In the case of the adverse scenario (+35%), the additional inflation would be close to 0.6 pp by early 2027, pushing inflation above 
4.6%. A shock of this magnitude would, in fact, reduce businesses’ ability to absorb rising costs, which would lead to price increases 
across a broader range of goods (food, industrial goods). Subsequently, the effect would only fade gradually.

Pascal Devaux 
Economist, Middle East, Western Balkans – Energy 
pascal.devaux@bnpparibas.com

Guillaume Derrien 
Economist, Europe, Eurozone, International Trade 
guillaume.a.derrien@bnpparibas.com

3 Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp, Europe’s main hub for the storage of raw materials.

## Document 383: Top of Mind - IPO Surge_ A Red Flag for Markets_

Source PDF: all report pdf/Top of Mind - IPO Surge_ A Red Flag for Markets_.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs (Top of Mind)
Date: 2026-07-28

Extraction status: ok

The Goldman Sachs Group, Inc.
The US IPO market has reopened in a big way in 2026, with issuance already at a
record high. But this activity has raised two key concerns for the equity market:
whether it’s flashing a la te-cycle warning sign and whether the market can
comfortably digest so much new supply. So, how worried should investors be? The
IPO Initiative’s Jay Ritter, Acadian’s Owen Lamont, and GS’ Ben Snider agree that
we’re not in an “IPO wave” (yet) but differ on ho w much of a red flag the surge in

issuance is signaling. Snider argues that the late-cycle warning signs of past IPO
booms aren’t evident today and digestion concerns are overdone. Ritter notes that
while high new issuance volume has historically predicted lower future returns, the
signal has been weak. But Lamont is more cautious, calling an IPO wave one of the
“four horsemen” of a bubble (especially alongside a surge in debt issuance), but
stresses that IPO waves don’t necessarily signal the market top. Key to watch: firs t

day IPO returns and the all-important AI outlook.
Ben Snider, Chief US Equity Strategist, Goldman Sachs
“I’m less worried [about the surge in new issuance] than the average investor… the late-cycle warning signs that have
historically accompanied IPO booms aren’t evident today… [and] the equity supply-demand balance this year looks better
than most investors intuit.”
Jay Ritter, Director of The IPO Initiative and Professor Emeritus, The University of Florida’s Warrington College

of Business
“High new issue volume is a predictor of low future market returns. But, like most indicators, this signal works only slightly
better than chance… So, I wouldn’t view an IPO boom on its own as a sign that a market downturn is imminent.”
Owen Lamont, Senior Vice President and Portfolio Manager, Acadian Asset Management
“Historically, issuance waves paired with capex waves… have generally resulted in disappointing returns for equity holders. So,

We provide a brief snapshot on the most important economies for the global markets

### Page 3

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 3 
Top of Mind Issue 150

After several relatively quiet years, the US IPO market has 
reopened in a big way in 2026, with issuance to date rising 
sharply to a new record high (and this isn’t just a US story—
see pg. 17). But this resurgence has raised two key concerns 
for the equity market: whether it’s flashing a late-cycle 
warning sign, and—even if it isn’t—whether the market can 
comfortably digest so much new issuance. So, how worried 
should investors be? 
We turn to several longtime market watchers for answers: 
Jay Ritter, Director of The IPO Initiative at the University of 
Florida’s Warrington College of Business, Owen Lamont, 
Senior Vice President and Portfolio Manager at Acadian Asset 
Management, and Ben Snider, GS Chief US Equity Strategist.  
We first ask whether the current moment constitutes an “IPO 
wave,” which has often preceded market downturns (see pg. 
8). All three agree that it doesn’t (at least not yet). Snider 
argues that while dollar volumes are already at record levels, 
the number of deals to date—which is much closer to the 
long-term average than past boom levels—suggests the 
current reopening is more of a normalization of IPO activity, 
amplified by a few large deals, than a true IPO boom. Ritter 
also points out that deal count remains fairly modest by 
historical standards owing to both cyclical and structural 
factors. And Lamont similarly notes that the market remains 
in an “IPO drought” by deal count, though he sees signs that 
an IPO and broader issuance wave may be forming. 
But with 2026 already a record issuance year, is this a red flag 
for the equity market? Snider says he is less concerned than 
the average investor, arguing that the late-cycle warning signs 
that have historically accompanied IPO booms aren’t evident 
today. In addition to the relatively unremarkable deal count, he 
notes that IPO valuations are only modestly above their long-
term average and well below prior boom levels. And Ritter 
adds that while high new issuance volume has been a past 
predictor of lower future market returns, the signal has been 
weak—working “only slightly better than chance.”  
But Lamont is more cautious. He acknowledges that higher 
issuance may simply reflect the capital demands of a 
transformative technology like AI. But he also points out that 
past bubbles have often been fueled by new technologies 
and have followed issuance and capex waves, as firms tend 
to sell equity when they believe it‘s overpriced. He therefore 
views an issuance wave as one of the “four horsemen” of a 
market bubble. That said, he stresses that IPO waves can 
last for years, so they “may mark the beginning of a bubble 
rather than the end.” And he also takes some comfort from 
the lack of extreme first day pops for recent IPOs, 
suggesting the absence of speculative euphoria. 
But perhaps the bigger concern is the market’s ability to 
digest the new issuance. Snider views such concerns as 
overdone given that the ~$700bn of corporate equity issuance 
he expects this year is modest relative to the size of the US 
equity market and the demand for US equities so far remains 
healthy. While he cautions that the supply-demand math will 
become more challenging in 2027 as investor lockups expire, 
he explains that some investors will choose to maintain their 
positions and those that don’t will likely buy other equities. He 
also notes a “self-limiting dynamic” to IPOs: they will come to 
market only as long as demand remains sufficient.   
Ritter also views digestion worries as overblown. He says 
that the $1.6tn of cash that US firms have annually returned 
to investors in recent years via dividends and buybacks must 
be recycled, so the IPO market—even with large deals—is 
absorbing just a fraction of the available capital. And Lamont 
notes that a threshold does exist at which increased supply 
will pressure prices. But he argues that where exactly it lies 
isn’t clear—for example, in the late 1990s, the market 
absorbed a large issuance wave for several years before 
prices began falling. (Note: the European equity market also 
faces indigestion concerns, but GS Global Equity Strategists 
Peter Oppenheimer and Guillaume Jaisson argue that 
Europe’s bigger challenge is a weak domestic equity bid, 
which they believe will constrain Europe’s IPO rebound).   
But adding to digestion concerns is the fact that rising 
issuance is not just an equity story. Firms are also turning to 
debt—primarily to fund the AI buildout—raising broader 
indigestion worries. Amanda Lynam, GS Chief Credit 
Strategist, argues that while the USD investment-grade 
market can absorb some of this debt issuance, issuer 
concentration and market saturation constraints will become 
more binding ahead, which she believes is underappreciated. 
Lamont, for his part, doesn’t view the AI-related debt boom 
as alarming on its own, but says the combination of a debt 
issuance wave and an equity issuance wave would be a 
negative sign for both credit and equity markets. 
Whether or not the rise in issuance is flashing warning signs 
for the broader market, investors must contend with the 
historical reality that IPOs tend to underperform in their first 
few years. So, how should investors navigate this? Lamont’s 
advice is simple: be patient. He says “IPOs are like bananas: 
they need to ripen before they’re ready to eat.” But Ritter 
cautions against generalizing the historical trend to the 
current IPO cohort. And Snider also argues for nuance, 
adding that investors can improve returns by owning a 
portfolio of IPOs rather than trying to pick a single winner. 
The performance question is even more important now that 
some major index providers have implemented new rules 
giving investors earlier access to large IPOs (see pg. 16). 
Ritter argues that earlier access makes sense for large new 
listings as long as it doesn’t create mechanical demand that 
distorts prices. But Lamont views the accelerated timeline 
more negatively, arguing that short seasoning periods could 
prevent proper price discovery.  
So, what should investors watch from here? Lamont is 
focused on whether the recent increase in activity becomes 
a wave and first day pops become more extreme. Snider is 
also watching first day returns as a key sentiment gauge as 
well as the all-important AI outlook—if it were to shift 
dramatically, he cautions that the outlook for both the 
market and associated IPOs would likely also shift.

IPO surge: a red flag for markets?

### Page 4

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 4 
Top of Mind Issue 150

Ben Snider is Chief US Equity Strategist at Goldman Sachs. Below, he argues that the current 
US IPO pickup reflects a return to normal rather than a true boom, with robust equity demand 
likely to absorb new supply and limit the risks to the broader market.  
Ashley Rhodes: How does this 
year’s reopening of the US IPO 
market compare to past IPO waves? 
Ben Snider: In dollar terms, 2026 is 
already a record year for the US IPO 
market. New issuance has totaled 
around $125bn year to date, 
surpassing the previous full-year 
record of around $120bn in 2021. We 
expect that momentum to continue, with total US IPO volume 
likely to exceed $200bn this year. 
However, in terms of the number of IPOs, the picture looks far 
less extreme. Roughly 60 US companies have gone public so 
far this year. While that represents a greater than 50% 
increase yoy and marks the strongest start to a year since 
2021, it is much closer to the long-term average than to past 
boom levels. The 25-year median of IPOs per year is around 
100, and the market is roughly on track for that in 2026. By 
comparison, over 250 companies went public in 2021, and 
nearly 400 did so in 1999 at the height of the Dot-Com Boom. 
So, while dollar volumes are at record levels, the current 
reopening looks more like a normalization of IPO activity—
amplified by a few very large deals—than the broad-based 
surge investors typically associate with an IPO boom. 
Ashley Rhodes: Does the concentration of large deals 
suggest that only the biggest, most mature companies can 
come to market? 
Ben Snider: No. The IPO market has reopened broadly, with 
deal activity happening across sectors—obviously including 
tech but also industrials and healthcare—as well as among 
smaller, earlier stage companies. But driving the outsized dollar 
volume to date is a handful of very large technology 
companies. So, the concentration of IPO dollars reflects a few 
very large deals and the strength of the tech/AI theme more 
than a market that’s only open to the largest firms. 
Ashley Rhodes: Why now for a pickup in IPO activity when 
the last several years have been relatively quiet? 
Ben Snider: More than any other factor, the macro backdrop 
has become more supportive after several challenging years. In 
2022, the sharpest Fed hiking cycle in history drove a sharp 
decline in valuations—particularly for the long-duration, high-
growth companies that typically represent the majority of the 
IPO pipeline—making it very difficult for many private firms to 
go public. Since then, interest rates have stopped rising, equity 
valuations have recovered, and both the economy and 
corporate profits have continued to grow. So, companies are 
getting back to the high-water mark of a few years ago in 
terms of total value, making going public more viable. Our IPO 
Barometer—which measures how conducive the macro 
environment is to new issuance—reflects this improvement. 
But, again, it’s indicating a more normal environment for IPO 
activity rather than boom conditions. 
Even so, the more supportive backdrop has also given private 
equity (PE) investors the opportunity to take a backlog of 
mature portfolio companies public. The motivation to do so has 
been in place for years given muted returns of capital and a 
growing desire for liquidity, but market conditions and 
valuations have been unfavorable. With a record $4tn in 
unrealized PE portfolio value and the IPO market now open for 
business, those investors are increasingly taking advantage. 
There is also a micro overlay to the pickup, tied to AI. The AI 
boom is, in many ways, a capex boom and the enormous 
funding required to build AI infrastructure has driven unusually 
large deal sizes among a relatively small number of companies. 
This dynamic is also driving a large increase in equity and debt 
issuance among established public companies. 
Ashley Rhodes: Would we be talking about an IPO boom 
at all if the AI phenomenon hadn’t taken hold? 
Ben Snider: Given the more supportive macro and market 
backdrop, we would likely still be experiencing the strongest 
IPO activity since 2021. But without the AI theme driving a 
handful of very large deals to date, dollar volumes would be 
much lower, and most investors would likely be characterizing 
the environment as a return to normal rather than as a boom. 
Ashley Rhodes: Despite that, many investors seem to be 
viewing the pickup in IPO activity as a warning sign for the 
equity market. What are investors’ main concerns? 
Ben Snider: A tremendous amount of investor concern exists, 
some of it stemming from the inclusion of IPO booms on the 
checklist of warning signs that typically come late in the cycle. 
In 1999 and 2021, elevated IPO activity coincided with 
stretched valuations and exuberant investor sentiment, and 
market downturns followed. More broadly, periods of heavy 
IPO activity have often preceded below-average equity market 
returns. So, investors are understandably wondering whether 
the current pickup is a sign of market froth or a potential peak. 
But the bigger and more universal investor concern revolves 
around the supply-demand balance in the equity market. The 
fear is that the substantial increase in equity supply will 
overwhelm the market and ultimately lead to lower prices. 
Ashley Rhodes: Are those concerns warranted? 
Ben Snider: I’m less worried than the average investor for a 
few reasons. First, the late-cycle warning signs that have 
historically accompanied IPO booms aren’t evident today. As 
we’ve discussed, IPO activity is rising, but it’s not extreme. 
And despite the relatively common narrative of stretched 
equity valuations, IPO valuations are only modestly above their 
long-term average and well below the elevated levels of prior 
IPO booms. To put some numbers on this, the median IPO last 
Interview with Ben Snider

### Page 5

El 
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 5 
Top of Mind Issue 150

year traded at 5x EV/sales versus the 30-year median of 
roughly 4x and compared to 9x in 1999 and 7x in 2021.  
Second, while the dollar amount of incremental supply is large, 
it’s modest relative to the overall size of the equity market. Our 
estimate for roughly $700bn of US corporate equity issuance in 
2026—including IPOs, follow-ons, converts, and SPACs—
equates to only around 1% of Russell 3000 market 
capitalization. This is in line with the 2015-2019 average and 
below the roughly 1.5% in 2021 and 2% at the height of the 
Dot-Com Boom.  
Third, data to date suggests that broader demand for US 
equities remains healthy. On the corporate side, S&P 500 
buybacks are up 4% yoy in 1Q26—continuing the recent trend 
of mid-single digit growth—and strategic M&A announcements 
have risen 100% yoy. On the household side, households 
were net sellers of equities during the Dot-Com Boom, but 
have shifted to net buyers in recent years via ETF and mutual 
fund inflows as well as robust retail trading activity. The share 
of the US equity market owned by foreign investors has also 
risen from 6% in 1995 to 18% today, with foreign investors 
continuing to add exposure despite last year’s pullback 
concerns. All told, the equity supply-demand balance this year 
looks better than most investors intuit. 
Ashley Rhodes: But aren’t corporate buybacks slowing? 
Ben Snider: A common misconception is that the AI capex 
buildout has essentially halted buybacks. It’s true that 
hyperscalers have pulled back on repurchases to fund their AI 
efforts, and buyback growth has slowed as a result. Scaling 
buybacks to market cap, the buyback yield is on track to be the 
lowest in around 15-20 years outside of recessions. But it’s still 
solidly positive, and, importantly, around 100bp higher than in 
the late 1990s. So, aggregate corporate demand remains 
healthy, with many companies outside of the hyperscalers—
particularly Banks and AI capex beneficiaries such as 
semiconductors—still expanding repurchase programs. Nvidia, 
for example, recently added $80bn to its buyback authorization, 
and total US buyback announcements are running at a record 
$960bn year to date. Looking ahead, we expect roughly $1.3tn 
in gross corporate buybacks in 2026, which should more than 
offset the incremental equity supply from both direct corporate 
issuance and potential post-lockup supply this year.  
Ashley Rhodes: How will the supply-demand picture shift 
ahead? 
Ben Snider: The supply-demand balance is clearly moving in 
the wrong direction, and the math admittedly becomes more 
challenging in 2027 as the lockups from this year’s IPOs 
expire. When a company goes public, typically only a portion of 
its shares becomes immediately available to trade. The rest 
remains in private hands, and those investors are restricted 
from selling in the public market for a designated period, 
traditionally for around six months after listing. Once that 
lockup period expires, those investors can sell, and the 
company’s public float—or the shares available for sale—can 
increase quite dramatically. That consideration may help 
explain why recent IPOs have struggled more than usual in 
their first few months of trading as some investors appear 
reluctant to buy before the final supply impact becomes clear.  
That said, concerns about the potential supply headwind are 
likely overdone. One reason is that unlocked shares don’t 
automatically translate into new public supply. Many investors 
will choose to maintain their ownership. And even when they 
do sell, some of the proceeds will likely be recycled back into 
other equities, creating at least a partial offset to new supply.  
Importantly, equity issuance also has a self-limiting dynamic. 
IPOs will continue to come to market only as long as demand 
remains sufficient, which requires equity prices and valuations 
to remain reasonably robust. If the market starts to struggle 
with too much supply, that will naturally limit future issuance 
and become self-correcting.  
Ashley Rhodes: Historically, IPOs tend to underperform the 
broader market for a few years after listing. Do you expect 
a similar pattern, and what are the investor implications?  
Ben Snider: While the median IPO has historically 
underperformed the broader market, the average IPO has 
outperformed the median, reflecting a right tail of firms that 
have delivered exceptionally strong returns. This is also why an 
equal-weighted portfolio of IPOs has generally outperformed 
the broader market over time—and why, if history repeats as I 
expect, investors are likely better served owning a portfolio of 
IPOs rather than trying to pick a single winner. Within that 
framework, investors should focus on the fundamental 
variables that have historically been linked to stronger post-IPO 
performance: valuation, revenue growth, and profitability. 
Firms that go public with lower multiples, higher revenue 
growth, and a shorter path to profitability typically post better 
returns. 
Ashley Rhodes: What risks could derail the IPO market’s 
current momentum? 
Ben Snider: Beyond the obvious macro risk of an economic 
slowdown, the more specific micro risk is the one we’ve been 
discussing: that the supply of new equity exceeds the market’s 
willingness to absorb it. A material deterioration in early post-
IPO performance would indicate that the market is struggling 
with the supply-demand balance. In that sense, first day 
returns will be important to watch—IPOs that fail to deliver 
initial first day gains may signal weaker demand and a less 
favorable outlook for new issuance. Likewise, consistently 
poor returns in the early weeks of trading would likely 
disincentivize investors and corporate managements from 
participating in future deals and serve as a self-regulating 
constraint on further issuance.  
It’s also important to acknowledge the role of AI. Investor 
appetite for AI-exposed stocks and surging AI capex spending 
have been major tailwinds for the broad equity market and are 
key reasons to expect more large private companies to go 
public. If the AI outlook were to change dramatically—whether 
due to earnings disappointments, a shift in capex expectations, 
or a broader reassessment of valuations—the outlook for both 
the market and associated IPOs would likely shift as well.
